Title: Quantum Simulation on Riemannian Manifolds

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.03673v1

Published Time: Mon, 05 Oct 2026 01:39:26 GMT

Number of Pages: 145

Markdown Content:
# Quantum Simulation on Riemannian Manifolds 

Dylan Herman *1, Jacob Watkins 1, Guneykan Ozgul 1, Jiayu Shen 1,Brandon Augustino 1, Junhyung Lyle Kim 1, and Shouvanik Chakrabarti †1 1Global Technology Applied Research, JPMorganChase, New York, NY 10001, USA October 5, 2026 

Abstract 

We investigate algorithms for the quantum simulation of the Schr ¨ odinger equation on a Riemannian manifold, where the kinetic operator is defined by the Laplace–Beltrami opera-tor corresponding to the metric. Our first algorithms are based on a global spectral method based on the identification of an efficient transform to the eigenbasis of the Laplace–Beltrami operator. We use this method to provide explicit, efficient, quantum simulation algorithms for the Riemannian Schr ¨ odinger equation on tori and spheres with their standard metrics, sim-plices with the Wright–Fisher metric, truncated positive orthants and their invertible affine images with the log-barrier Hessian metric, and ℓp balls with a metric induced by the Duffy map. Our second algorithm is based on a coherent simulation of local spectral methods on multiple charts, and is in principle applicable to any compact manifold. We first analyze this algorithm in the continuum and derive conditions under which a polynomial spectral cutoff suffices. We also provide a discretization analysis of a polynomial spectral cutoff for tensor-products of constant-dimensional manifolds. Finally, we consider applications of these meth-ods to optimization and physical simulation. For optimization, we provide results including a generalization and convergence analysis of Quantum Hamiltonian Descent for geodesically convex functions that leads to explicit algorithms on the sphere and simplex, and a Rieman-nian generalization of the Real-Space Adiabatic Algorithm. For physical simulation, we show that our algorithms can simulate certain spatially discretized field theories, including a variant of the nonlinear sigma model.

Quantum simulation is the paradigmatic application of quantum computers and was the primary impetus for the interest in this form of computing [Fey81]. The study of explicit algorithms for quantum simulation was initiated by Lloyd [Llo96], and over time many powerful quantum algo-rithms have been developed for the simulation of appropriately specified Hamiltonians that act on a discrete space, using techniques that include product formulae [Llo96; Chi+21], linear com-binations of unitaries [CW12; BCK15], or qubitization and quantum signal processing for block-encoded Hamiltonians [LC17; LC19; Gil+19]. There has also been significant interest in simulating Schr ¨ odinger dynamics in real space. This problem takes the schematic form 

i∂tψ(t) = H(t)ψ(t), H(t) = −a(t)∆ + b(t) f , (1) on a Hilbert space such as L2(Ω, d x), where Ω is a bounded subset of Rd. Simulating such sys-tems requires appropriate discrete representations of the continuum operators. This continuum-to-circuit passage must be an explicit part of the algorithmic analysis in real space, since the choice of discretization method, and the resulting grid spacing, basis cutoff, smoothing proce-dure, quadrature rule, and aliasing analysis can all affect the number of qubits, and the query and gate complexity. Kivlichan et al. bounded real-space simulation costs using finite-difference discretizations [Kiv+17], Childs et al. [Chi+22] gave an explicit Fourier pseudospectral framework for real-space dynamics, and Chakrabarti et al. gave a refined analysis of this method and concrete estimates for Lipschitz-continuous potentials [Cha+26]. In addition to the natural physics applications, another motivation for real-space simulation comes from quantum algorithms for continuous optimization that use quantum dynamics as the algorithmic foundation, with the objective function encoded in the potential. A systematic study of such algorithms was initiated by Leng et al. under the name “Quantum Hamiltonian De-scent” [Len+23]. Subsequently, quantum dynamical algorithms have been explored for linear pro-gramming [Aug+23a], convex optimization [CSW25; Len+25b; Cha+26; Gri+25], and non-convex optimization [LSL23; LZW23; Che+25; Len+25a; Her+25]. It is notable that in several regimes, the strongest known quantum speedups are achieved by dynamical methods rather than by more tra-ditional quantum optimization primitives. Examples include the best-known speedups for high-dimensional stochastic convex optimization [Cha+26], worst-case query speedups for constructed nonconvex optimization instances [Len+25a], and speedups over known classical algorithms for specific explicit families of nonconvex functions [Her+25]. An important generalization is that of quantum simulation over a Riemannian manifold (M, g).In this setting, the corresponding kinetic operator is not the standard Laplacian but rather the Laplace–Beltrami operator, expressed in local coordinates as, 

∆gu = ρ−1∂i

ρgij ∂ju



, where ρ(x) =

det (gij (x)) , (2) where repeated coordinate indices are summed according to the Einstein summation convention. The target for simulation is now the Riemannian Schr ¨ odinger equation 

i∂tψ(t) = Hg(t)ψ(t), Hg(t) = −a(t)∆g + b(t) f , ψ(t) ∈ L2(M, d vol g) (3) where d vol g(x) = ρ(x) dx. One recovers the Euclidean case by setting g = I in local coordinates. 3Why should we care about simulating such dynamics? Physics provides a direct motivation. A particle subject to regular holonomic constraints, or a rigid rotor, has a smooth Riemannian config-uration space. Its Hamiltonian uses the corresponding Laplace–Beltrami operator for the kinetic term, potentially supplemented by a scalar-curvature term from the quantization prescription or by an extrinsic-curvature potential generated when the constraint is realized through strong confinement [DeW57; Cos81a]. These elementary examples are usually low-dimensional. For the high-dimensional setting where speedups from quantum computing are most important, the more relevant examples arise from spatially discretized field theories. A standard nonlinear sigma model with target T has lattice configuration space T|Λ|; its kinetic term is a sum of target-space Laplacians, while the spatial-gradient and on-site terms act as a multiplication potential. This includes the O(N) models with T = SN−1 and the CPN−1 models with the Fubini–Study met-ric [Bur00; Ale+19; Sin22; Eic78; dLD78]. Hamiltonian lattice gauge theory with compact gauge group G similarly has kinematic configuration space G|E | , with one factor for each lattice link; the electric term is a sum of group Laplacians, while the magnetic plaquette term is a multiplication potential [KS75; Ba ˜ n+20]. In both cases, the configuration-space dimension grows with the num-ber of lattice sites or links even when each factor has fixed dimension. On any fixed spatial lattice, these Hamiltonians are therefore exact instances of the Riemannian Schr ¨ odinger equation above. A second motivation for non-Euclidean geometry comes from classical optimization, where geometry can be part of both the problem and the algorithm. Constrained optimization is a cen-tral example. When equality constraints define a smooth feasible manifold, Riemannian meth-ods use tangent directions and retractions to preserve feasibility. Spheres, Stiefel and Grassmann manifolds, and fixed-rank matrix manifolds are typical cases [Bou23]. More broadly, mirror de-scent [NY83; Bub15] replaces Euclidean geometry with the Bregman geometry of a chosen mirror map, which can be selected to reflect problem structure; negative entropy on the probability sim-plex is a canonical example [BT03]. In interior-point methods, the geometry induced by the Hes-sian of a self-concordant barrier is more fundamental still: it determines the local norm in which the Newton direction is defined, its unit ball is a Dikin ellipsoid contained in the feasible region, and self-concordance controls how quickly this geometry changes from one point to the next. These properties allow Newton steps to track the central path and underlie the polynomial iteration bounds for path-following methods [NN94; Ren01]. Non-Euclidean geometry plays many important roles beyond enforcing constraints. Natural-gradient and related methods use a position-dependent metric as a preconditioner to reduce sensitivity to parametrization and anisotropic scaling [Ama98]; when the objective is geodesi-cally convex, the chosen geometry can additionally support global first-order convergence guar-antees [ZS16]. Thus non-Euclidean geometry can encode feasibility, exploit symmetry, and im-prove conditioning. Geometry also plays an algorithmic role in sampling, which in turn under-lies simulated-annealing methods for convex optimization and algorithms for volume compu-tation [AH16; BK22]. Riemannian Hamiltonian Monte Carlo adapts its trajectories to the local scaling and correlation structure of a target distribution, and has led to improved worst-case mix-ing bounds and faster volume algorithms for polytopes [GC11; LV22; LV18], as well as practical gains on high-dimensional, ill-conditioned problems [Koo+22]. The quantum-dynamical speedups for convex and nonconvex optimization highlighted earlier concern unconstrained problems: the dynamics uses a Euclidean kinetic operator and encodes the objective in the potential [Cha+26; Len+25a; Her+25]. To extend this approach to constrained optimization while preserving feasibility by construction, the kinetic evolution must also respect 4the feasible set. For a smooth feasible manifold (M, g), this means replacing −∆ by −∆g; for an inequality-constrained domain, a barrier-induced metric can similarly encode the geometry of its interior. Gribling, Apers, Nieuwboer, and Walter [Gri+25] make this connection explicit for a self-concordant barrier Φ: they equip the feasible domain with the Hessian metric g = ∇2Φ

and study central-path Schr ¨ odinger operators built from −∆g. They prove spectral-gap bounds without the Euclidean condition-number dependence and, under assumptions that include access to the corresponding Riemannian evolution, use these bounds to formulate a quantum interior-point method. Efficient digital simulation of Riemannian Schr ¨ odinger dynamics is therefore a concrete primitive for extending quantum-dynamical optimization to constrained problems. Finally, we note that the simulation of (3) is generally understood to be significantly more complicated than that of simulating that standard real-space dynamics. In flat real-space simu-lation, the Fourier transform gives a global basis in which the kinetic energy is simple, while a position grid gives a representation in which the potential is simple. For a general manifold, such a transform requires an efficiently implementable map to the eigenbasis of the Laplace–Beltrami operator, and simulation requires us to both identify such a map and develop an efficient quan-tum algorithm for the transform. All the nuances of the discretization analysis in the flat space must also be handled again, and this analysis may in general be more complicated. The situation is even more complex for a general manifold: there may be no single global coordinate chart, no efficiently implementable transform to Laplace–Beltrami eigenfunctions, and no uniform volume form. In such cases, one can hope to use local methods where Euclidean simulation methods ap-ply for each local chart, however this brings additional difficulties: Local charts may overlap and amplitudes on overlaps must be recombined coherently rather than treated as independent clas-sical alternatives. Additionally, the continuum-to-circuit passage causes inconsistencies among different charts. As a consequence, the simulation of Riemannian Schr ¨ odinger operators has been explicitly left open by papers such as [Gri+25]. The main goal of this paper is to make the first substantial algorithmic progress towards resolving this question. 

1.1 Results 

Our main results make a systematic analysis of simulation algorithms for Riemannian Schr ¨ odinger equations. In (3), the Hamiltonian contains differential and multiplication operators acting on wavefunction ψ(t) defined continuously on M. A digital algorithm must first choose a finite rep-resentation of the wavefunction and a finite matrix representing these operators. The analogous problem on a periodic Euclidean domain is handled by the Fourier pseudospectral method. One truncates the Fourier expansion of the wavefunction, uses that the Laplacian is diagonal in the Fourier basis, and applies the discrete Fourier transform to evaluate the potential on a finite set of quadrature nodes. Since the digital evolution does not need to agree with the continuous evo-lution, a rigorous pseudospectral method provides an error guarantee for a chosen Fourier cutoff and the quadrature rule that determine the space in which digital evolution takes place and how accurately the potential is represented respectively. 

Global Spectral Methods. On a Riemannian manifold, the pseduospectral method can be ex-tended by forming an analogous basis using the eigenfunctions of the Laplace–Beltrami operator. We call this approach global spectral method where one relies on a single global transform encod-ing the geometry of the manifold. This approach becomes viable when one is be able implement 5this basis transform and approximate the potential in this transformed space using the quadra-ture rules. Similar to the Euclidean case, one needs to determine the right cutoff threshold for the spectral degree and the quadrature rules to provide a rigorous simulation guarantee. The main additional issue is that in Euclidean case, multiplication by a function with maximum spectral degree K, increases the degree of the wavefunction by at most K. This does not hold for gen-eral manifolds, however for the manifolds considered in this paper, each such multiplication can increase spectral degree by only a controlled amount due to slow spectral growth property. Using the global spectral method, we give end-to-end digital simulation algorithms with rigor-ous guarantee for several important settings. These include flat torus, the sphere with its standard metric, and the simplex with the Wright–Fisher metric. We also obtain algorithms for positive or-thants, affine images of such domains, and ℓp balls by reducing their chosen metrics to flat periodic problems through explicit coordinate changes. 

General Manifolds and Coherent-Chart Methods. The need for an efficiently implementable global transform and the explicit global spectral structure limits the applicability of the global spectral method. A compact manifold always has a Laplace–Beltrami eigenbasis, but this fact alone does not provide slow spectral growth, useful quadrature rules, or efficient quantum trans-forms. To address this limitation, we also introduce the coherent-chart method that does not rely on one single global basis transformation. Since a manifold resembles an Euclidean space in the near vicinity of a point, one can decompose the space into local charts and use the ordinary Fourier ex-pansion similar to the pseudospectral method for each chart. To be able to this, one first needs to provide a localization procedure that allows the wavefunction to be decomposed into local charts that can be simulated efficiently by retaining the relative phases so that it can be recombined into original global state. We give a localization procedure inspired by the well known IMS formula. The Riemannian IMS identity allows to decompose the wavefunction and the Hamiltonian into local charts and also provide the right correction term to the potential that allows the local opera-tors recombine into right global form. We give simulation guarantees for the resulting algorithm under explicit Sobolev and geometric regularity assumptions that are required to control the over-lapping regions between the local charts. The resulting approximation is controlled by a local Fourier cutoff: in each chart, only Fourier modes whose coordinate frequencies below the cut-off are retained. For general compact manifolds, we bound the difference between the exact and truncated continuum evolutions, and show that a cutoff that is polynomial in the dimension suf-fices at fixed accuracy under explicit geometric and Sobolev assumptions. For tensor products of constant-dimensional manifolds, we extend the analysis to a finite-dimensional discretization, us-ing a global interpolation space to connect the local grids, and prove a corresponding polynomial cutoff under various interpolation and regularity assumptions. We highlight that the two sets of simulation results differ in the scope of their primary con-tribution. In the case of global spectral methods, the simulation algorithm is clear as it is an extension of the pseudospectral algorithm used in flat space. Our main contribution there is to ob-tain explicit end-to-end algorithms with rigorous guarantees and resource estimates, for particular well-studied manifolds of application-specific interest. On the other hand for general manifolds, to the best of our knowledge, there did not exist an algorithmic framework that is conducive to analysis. Our contribution is to propose such a framework and determine conditions under which the truncation requirements (which are one of the main bottlenecks in the analysis of simulation algorithms) are benign. 6Table 1 summarizes the scope and status of the results. The following subsections give a more detailed overview of the two simulation methods. Finally, Section 1.1.3 gives an overview of our study of applications of quantum simulation on Riemanninan manifolds, and discusses how our algorithms fit into these applications.

1.1.1 Global spectral methods 

The global spectral method represents the wavefunction by its coefficients in finitely many eigen-functions of the Laplace–Beltrami operator on the whole manifold. We introduce a binning func-tion for the eigenfunctions L : I → N. We define the ℓ-th level to be 

Hℓ := span {Yα : L(α) = ℓ}, VN :=

> NM
> ℓ=0

Hℓ.Let {Yα}α∈I be an orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions for −∆g. Consider a function L : I → N

that bins the eigenfunctions into spaces Hℓ := span {Yα : L(α) = ℓ}, with VN = H0 ⊕ · · · ⊕ H N .The kinetic term is diagonal in this basis and it multiplies each coefficient with a phase. The poten-tial instead acts by pointwise multiplication of f . We apply it by transforming the wavefunction to weighted values on a quadrature grid, querying f at the grid points, and transforming back. The method therefore requires a cutoff N for which the evolution restricted to VN approximates the continuum evolution, and a quantum circuit that maps coefficients in VN to weighted function values on the grid. For example, Yk(x) = e2πik ·x, L(k) = ∥k∥∞ corresponds to the Fourier spectral method. 7This approach has two distinct sources of approximation error. The first comes from restricting the wavefunction to VN . Multiplication by the potential generally produces components outside 

VN , even when the initial state has low degree. The second comes from the quadrature rule. A digital implementation replaces the integrals that define the matrix elements of the potential by finite weighted sums over the grid. If the grid cannot resolve a component produced by the potential, that component is mistaken for a combination of retained eigenfunctions. This error is called aliasing error , and previous analyses of real-space simulation algorithms control aliasing through high-order regularity bounds on the evolving wavefunction [Cha+26]. For black-box Lipschitz potentials, the grid resolution obtained from such bounds can grow exponentially with the dimension. Our analysis instead replaces the potential by a function fK ∈ V K that approximates f uni-formly. This replacement appears only in the analysis and our algorithm still queries f . The the evolutions generated by f and by fK differ by an amount proportional to ∥ f − fK ∥∞. The analysis then uses slow spectral growth of the eigenfunctions: 

Hℓ1 Hℓ2 ⊆ V ℓ1+ℓ2 . (4) This condition states the product of eigenfunctions Yα, Yβ with α ∈ L−1(ℓ1) and β ∈ L−1(ℓ2)

has no component Yγ with L(γ) > ℓ 1 + ℓ2. Consequently, each multiplication by fK raises the 

L-index of the wavefunction by at most K. If the initial state lies in VL, the term of the interaction-picture Dyson series with q applications of fK lies in VL+qK , and the norms of these terms decrease with q. Choosing the cutoff N large enough to contain the terms of low order therefore bounds the truncation error without estimating derivatives of the evolving wavefunction. Slow spectral growth also determines how many quadrature points are needed. A matrix el-ement of fK between two eigenfunctions in VN is the integral of a product of three functions of known degree, so the integrand lies in V2N+K. A quadrature rule that integrates exactly every function in V2N+K therefore reproduces the matrix of fK on VN without aliasing error. This ap-proach is called oversampling , because it uses more points than are needed to represent a state in 

VN . The total error then consists of the uniform approximation error ∥ f − fK ∥∞ and the contribu-tion of the terms of high order in the Dyson series; See Corollary 3.6. We first give explicit constructions for torus, sphere and simplex. On the torus, averaging over a finite Fourier kernel approximates the potential, and a padded quantum Fourier transform changes representations. On the sphere, evolving the potential for a short time under the heat equation suppresses high spherical harmonics while changing a Lipschitz function only slightly. The recursive structure of spherical harmonics then reduces the spectral transform to a sequence of one-dimensional polynomial transforms. On the simplex, averaging over multinomial samples produces a finite-degree polynomial approximation, while a recursive factorization of the global polynomial eigenfunctions provides the corresponding transform. The sphere and simplex trans-forms have polynomial rather than polylogarithmic dependence on the one-dimensional cutoff, but the discretization theorems keep that cutoff polynomial in the relevant problem parameters; see Theorems 3.17, 3.18, 3.22, and 3.23. The second group of examples consists of constrained domains from optimization. The posi-tive orthant encodes positivity constraints, its invertible affine images encode systems of d linearly independent linear inequalities, and ℓp balls encode norm constraints. On these domains, an ex-plicit change of coordinates makes the metric Euclidean. On the positive orthant, logarithmic coordinates transform the Laplace–Beltrami operator of the log-barrier Hessian metric into the 8Euclidean Laplacian, so simulation on a truncated orthant reduces to simulation on a box. On a truncated ℓp domain, the Duffy map, a triangular change of coordinates, gives the same reduction for a metric that we define through this map. The global results therefore use two kinds of structure. On the torus, sphere, and simplex, the eigenfunctions satisfy slow spectral growth, the potential has explicit uniform approximations by finitely many eigenfunctions, and the quantum transforms are built from explicit quadrature rules. On the orthant and ℓp domains, a change of coordinates makes the metric Euclidean. 

1.1.2 Coherent-chart methods 

The coherent-chart method applies when no tractable transform to a global eigenbasis of −∆g

is available. Instead of expanding the wavefunction in global eigenfunctions, we use ordinary Fourier expansions in local coordinate boxes. To avoid discontinuities when a local function is extended periodically across the boundary of its box, we localize the wavefunction with a smooth quadratic partition of unity {χℓ}ℓ. Each χℓ is supported away from the boundary of the corre-sponding box, and ∑ℓ χ2 

> ℓ

= 1. The localized function χℓu therefore vanishes near the boundary and has a periodic Fourier expansion in that chart. Because coordinate charts overlap, the same global amplitude can appear in several localized functions. These functions must remain as components of one quantum state so that their am-plitudes interfere correctly when they are mapped back to the manifold. In the ℓth chart, we first multiply by χℓ, then express the result in the coordinates y = κℓ(x), and finally multiply by 

ρℓ(y)1/2 , where ρℓ(y) dy is the Riemannian volume measure in those coordinates. The last oper-ation converts the local weighted L2 norm to the ordinary Lebesgue L2 norm. These operations define the isometry 

(T u)ℓ(y) = ρℓ(y)1/2 χℓ(κ−1 

> ℓ

(y)) .The adjoint T † maps the local functions back to the manifold and adds their amplitudes on chart overlaps. Thus T provides a coherent local representation of one global wavefunction. The main obstacle is that localizing the wavefunction also changes its kinetic energy. If one applies −∆g separately to the functions χℓu and then recombines them, the derivatives act on both u and χℓ. The resulting extra terms prevent the uncorrected local operators from reproducing 

−∆g. The Riemannian IMS identity computes their total contribution exactly:

measures how rapidly the cutoff changes across the manifold. The po-tential commutes with the cutoff functions, so no analogous correction is needed for the potential term. We subtract the full IMS potential WIMS from every local kinetic block. After converting the local blocks to flat measure, their corrected direct sum eH⊕(t) satisfies 

H(t) = T † eH⊕(t)T .The IMS correction therefore turns the collection of local differential operators into an exact repre-sentation of the global Riemannian Hamiltonian before any Fourier truncation is introduced. A spectral simulation replaces an infinite expansion by finitely many modes, and the cutoff determines both the approximation error and the resources needed to represent the truncated op-erator. An algorithm that retains coordinate frequencies through N requires O(d log N) qubits for 9the local Fourier labels. The largest retained derivatives also grow with N, so the normalization and simulation cost of the local kinetic blocks depend on the same parameter. A useful conver-gence result must therefore show that the target accuracy can be reached without requiring the cutoff to grow exponentially with the dimension. We analyze the cutoff in two stages. First, for a general compact manifold, we give condi-tions ensuring that the energy cutoff required to truncate the continuum model to local Fourier modes grows at most polynomially with the manifold dimension. Second, for products of fixed-dimensional manifolds, we give conditions ensuring that the retained continuum modes admit a finite-dimensional discretization with polynomial local resolution. 

Polynomial cutoff in the continuum. We begin with an analysis of the algorithm in the contin-uum. In each chart, the projection PN retains the Fourier modes with ∥k∥∞ ≤ N and discards all higher coordinate frequencies. Applying this projection before and after each local operator restricts the input to the retained modes and removes higher frequencies created by the variable coefficients. Theorem 4.9 quantifies how well the truncated local operators approximate the exact evolu-tion. While we refer to the theorem for the precise regularity conditions, the error is controlled when the evolving wavefunction has sufficiently many square-integrable derivatives, and when the change of coordinates, metric coefficients, volume densities, partition of unity, potential, and IMS correction have bounded derivatives through the same order. These conditions ensure that the high-frequency components discarded by PN decrease as N grows. If the number of con-trolled derivatives is proportional to d and the corresponding derivative bounds grow no faster than poly (d)d, Corollary 4.10 shows that N = poly (d) suffices for a fixed accuracy. 

Polynomial cutoff after discretization. A finite-dimensional implementation introduces a sec-ond problem: grids belonging to overlapping charts generally represent different points of the manifold. For a product of constant-dimensional manifolds M = ∏m 

> ν=1

Mν, we connect the local grids through finite source spaces defined separately on the factors. Choose a finite basis {L(ν) 

> q

}q

for the source space on each factor. Products of these basis functions form a basis for the global source space, so their coefficients define one global function. Evaluation maps send these global coefficients to the values of that function on each chart grid. A local discrete Fourier transform converts the sampled values to Fourier coefficients, the local Hamiltonian acts in the chart repre-sentation, and the adjoint evaluation maps return the result to the global coefficients. Because the source bases and evaluation maps factor across the manifolds Mν, these operations can be applied factor by factor on constant-dimensional (for an appropriately chosen source interpolation basis) registers. The potential may still couple all factors through its value at a product-grid point. Theorem 4.23 analyzes the resulting finite-dimensional evolution. The result assumes that the geometry of each constant-dimensional factor remains uniformly smooth as the number of fac-tors grows. It also requires source spaces that approximate sufficiently smooth functions without introducing an exponentially larger resolution, and it requires the exact and intermediate wave-functions in the error analysis to remain sufficiently differentiable. Under the precise bounds stated in the theorem, with constants C0 and β independent of d and N, satisfy sup 

> 0≤t≤T

1.1.3 Applications 

Section 5 studies physical and optimization problems can be expressed as evolution under a Rie-mannian Schr ¨ odinger operator. For these applications, we give details that illustrate the uplift that can be obtained by quantum simulation on quantum manifolds. For many of the highlighted set-tings, the geometry and boundary in question is compatible with one of the end-to-end simulation algorithm given in this paper. However, we also describe some more general cases. Most of the applications below should not be interpreted as complete end-to-end quantum speedups for the surrounding task. Firstly, in addition to a simulation procedure, an end-to-end algorithm might also require the analysis of state preparation, measurement procedure, spectral gaps, or other convergence guarantees. We therefore distinguish applications that identify Rie-mannian Schr ¨ odinger evolution as a required subroutine, from settings where the end-to-end algorithm follows naturally. Secondly, we do not make an effort in this paper to optimize the polynomially scaling resources required by our simulation algorithms (a notable exception is our analysis for simulation on the torus which is the most efficient that we have found in the litera-ture.) Therefore, even when a natural end-to-end algorithm arises from our results, it does not immediately yield a quantum speedup. Finally, a detailed comparison with classical algorithms is well beyond the scope of this paper. 

Physical simulation. Section 5.1 shows that several well-studied physical systems can be written as Riemannian Schr ¨ odinger equations on high-dimensional configuration spaces. The examples include quantum mechanics on curved spaces, nonlinear sigma models with general target man-ifolds, CPN−1 models, and Hamiltonian lattice gauge theories. After spatial discretization, the field-theory Hamiltonians act on products of the target manifold or gauge group, with a sum of Laplace–Beltrami operators as the kinetic term and the interactions as a multiplication potential. We then apply the sphere algorithm to a spatially discretized O(N) nonlinear sigma model with a nearest-neighbor chordal interaction in Section 5.1.5. Theorem 5.1 gives an end-to-end digital simulation with explicit bounds on the state error and on the query, gate, and qubit costs. 

Interior-point methods. Section 5.2 discusses the quantum path-following method of Gribling, Apers, Nieuwboer, and Walter [Gri+25], which uses Schr ¨ odinger operators whose kinetic term is the Laplace–Beltrami operator of a self-concordant barrier metric. Their algorithm assumes access to controlled evolution under these operators. For the standard logarithmic barrier on the positive orthant, Theorem 3.11 supplies a digital simulation of this evolution after the domain is truncated to a compact box. The result therefore implements one of the central algorithmic primitives required by that method. 

Quantum Hamiltonian Descent. The QHD algorithm [Len+23] performs optimization by evolv-ing a wavefunction under a time-dependent Schr ¨ odinger equation whose potential is the objective function. Its Euclidean analysis uses a Lyapunov function to prove convergence for convex poten-tials. Theorem 5.6 in Section 5.3 extends this analysis to Riemannian manifolds and geodesically convex potentials. The sphere and simplex specializations in Theorems 5.8 and 5.10 combine with 11 our digital simulation algorithms to give end-to-end optimization algorithms for geodesically con-vex functions on those manifolds. 

Real-Space Adiabatic Algorithm. Herman et al. [Her+25] introduced the Real-Space Adiabatic Algorithm algorithm to study quantum speedups for structured nonconvex optimization prob-lems in Euclidean space. The algorithm begins from the ground state of a kinetic operator and adiabatically turns on the objective potential, so that the final ground state concentrates near the minimizers. In [Her+25], the authors identify mechanisms by which this algorithm offers large quantum speedups over known classical algorithms. Section 5.4 defines the corresponding Rie-mannian algorithm by replacing the Euclidean Laplacian with −∆g. Theorem 5.12 gives the adia-batic guarantee, and Theorem 5.13 gives a digital implementation on a periodic Duffy ℓp domain. The Duffy map makes this adiabatic path unitarily equivalent to the corresponding path on a Euclidean box, so the mechanisms identified by Herman et al. carry over to these constrained problems. 

1.2 Related work 

Real-space simulation on flat domains. Digital simulation of the time-dependent Schr ¨ odinger equation on a position grid goes back to Wiesner and Zalka [Wie96; Zal98]. Kassal et al. used the grid-based split-operator approach, which alternates between position and momentum rep-resentations via the quantum Fourier transform, to simulate chemical dynamics [Kas+08]. First-quantized plane-wave algorithms later achieved sublinear scaling in the basis size [Bab+19] and explicit fault-tolerant resource estimates for chemistry [Su+21]. The analyses discussed in the in-troduction [Kiv+17; Chi+22; Cha+26] all bound the discretization error through the regularity of the evolving wavefunction, either assumed or, in the black-box Lipschitz setting, proved. Our global spectral method takes a different approach: aliasing is removed exactly for a finite-mode comparison potential, so no high-order regularity bound on the evolution is needed. On the flat torus, this yields a register of O(d log Q) qubits with Q = poly (d, 1/ ϵ) (Theorem 3.8). 

Quantum simulation on curved configuration spaces in physics. Several physical models al-ready have curved configuration spaces, and their quantum simulation has been studied directly. For O(N) nonlinear sigma models, proposals include an analog laboratory simulator for the O(3)

model [SM05], a digital scheme that replaces the target sphere by the fuzzy sphere while pre-serving the exact O(3) symmetry [Ale+19], and qubit regularizations with few qubits per site that reproduce the relevant critical behavior [SC19; Sin22]; Hamiltonian lattice gauge theories raise the same questions for compact gauge groups [Ba ˜ n+20]. These works construct model-specific finite Hilbert spaces and justify them through universality arguments supported by numerics, rather than through bounds on the error relative to the continuum dynamics. On the contrary, we provide a general construction that applies to any compact manifold with the required structure, together with explicit bounds on the error relative to the continuum Schr ¨ odinger evolution with a black-box potential. 

Quantum dynamical optimization with geometry. Beyond the works mentioned in the intro-duction, Quantum Hamiltonian Descent now includes stochastic [Pen+25] and gradient-augmented [LS25] variants, as well as fault-tolerant resource estimates [Liu+26]. In all of these, the kinetic 12 term is Euclidean. Constraints have so far been handled outside the dynamics, for instance by embedding QHD in an augmented-Lagrangian loop that solves a sequence of unconstrained sub-problems [LFH25; Wu+26]. Two works put geometry into the kinetic term: Gribling et al. [Gri+25] assume access to the Riemannian evolution, and Abe and Nagai [AN26] assume sparse access to a chosen discretization of the Laplace–Beltrami operator; neither constructs these objects or bounds the resulting error. Our algorithms supply this step for the manifolds in Table 1; Section 5 discusses the consequences for optimization in more detail. To our knowledge, the global spectral methods of this paper are the first digital quantum al-gorithms with end-to-end discretization error bounds for Schr ¨ odinger dynamics with a black-box potential on non-flat configuration spaces, and the coherent-chart method extends the continuum convergence analysis to general compact manifolds under explicit geometric and regularity as-sumptions. 

# 2 Preliminaries and Notation 

Riemannian geometry is a rich topic of mathematics that we cannot hope to cover in detail here. See [Lee18] for an excellent introduction. We will merely establish some basic notation and results relevant to this work. Throughout this work, the natural numbers N are defined with 0 included. A manifold is a Hausdorff, second countable topological space M, which is locally Euclidean, in the sense that there exists an atlas {(Uα, κα)}α∈I , where {Uα}α∈I is an open cover, and 

κα : Uα → Ωα ⊆ Rd (5) is a homeomorphism defining local coordinates. Here d ∈ Z+ is the manifold dimension. M is, more specifically, a smooth manifold when the transition maps 

ταβ = κα ◦ κ−1 

> β

. (6) are diffeomorphisms whenever Uα ∩ Uβ is not empty. For finite-dimensional smooth manifolds, it is known that there exists a smooth atlas containing at most d + 1 charts [Sol74]. From computa-tional purposes, assuming a finite set of charts is natural and perhaps necessary. In this work, all atlases are finite , and one can take I = [ nch ] for some nch ∈ Z+. Smoothness allows for the con-struction of tangent spaces T pM at each point x ∈ M , and the disjoint union of these d-dimensional vector spaces defines the tangent bundle TM.A Riemannian manifold (M, g) is a smooth manifold M endowed with a metric g. This metric is a smooth, positive definite symmetric (0, 2 )-tensor field, which defines an inner product at each 

p ∈ M via 

⟨w, v⟩g := gp(w, v), w, v ∈ TpM. (7) Local coordinates computations via the atlas provide a convenient setting for calculation, and are indicated by use of indices with appropriate subscript or superscript. We denote the metric in coordinates as gij and inverse metric is given by gij . Einstein summation notation is used for brevity. As the name suggests, the metric provides a local notion of distance, and encodes geometrical properties of the space such as curvature, volume, and shortest paths between points. Let us now define those relevant to this work. Given a smooth vector field v, define the scalar field 13 |v|g := pg(v, v). Let |g| := det (gij ). In local coordinates x = ( x1, . . . , xd), the natural, coordinate-invariant volume measure on M is then dvol g(x) := ρ(x)dx , ρ(x) :=

|g(x)| (8) where dx ≡ dx 1 . . . dx d is the volume form. This can be used to define the Hilbert space of wave-functions on M.

Let exp x : TxM → M be the exponential map, which sends a tangent vector v ∈ TxM to the point reached at time 1 by the geodesic starting at x with initial velocity v. Wherever exp x is one-to-one along the minimizing geodesic from x to y, the Riemannian logarithm log x : Ux → Tx M is defined as its local inverse. log x (y) = v ⇐⇒ exp x(v) = y (13) Equivalently, if γ ∈ C(x, y) is the unique minimizing geodesic from x to y, then log x (y) = ˙γ(0), | log x (y)|g = dg(x, y). (14) In short, log x(y) naturally generalizes the idea of the vector y − x in Euclidean space, which points from x to y and whose length is the distance. when x̸ = y. We emphasize that this logarithm function is defined only under the provided assumptions of unique geodesics. A particular setting where this occurs is for geodesically convex sets. 14 Definition 2.1 (Geodesic convexity) . A subset C of a Riemannian manifold (M, g) is geodesically con-vex if for each p , q ∈ S, there exists a unique minimizing geodesic segment from p to q, whose image lies entirely in C.

Geodesically convex sets always exists: take, for instance, sufficiently small geodesic balls. In con-tinuous optimization, convexity of a function is a useful and often practical assumption that allows for efficient gradient-based algorithms. See [Bub15] or other standard sources for background and basic definitions. A natural generalization to manifolds is as follows. 

Definition 2.2 (Geodesically convex functions) . For C geodesically convex, a function f : C → R is geodesically convex if, for every unit-velocity geodesic arc γ contained in C, the composition f ◦ γ is convex as a real function. 

Proposition 2.3 (Geodesic convexity, differentiable function) . For C geodesically convex, a differen-tiable function f : C → R is geodesically convex if and only if for every x , y ∈ C connected by a geodesic: f (y) ≥ f (x) + ⟨∇ f (x), log x (y)⟩g

We say that a function f : M → R is geodesically G-Lipschitz if it is G-Lipschitz continuous with respect to dg.A useful identity for bounding the difference in two Hamiltonian evolutions is given by Duhamel’s formula.

# 3 Global Spectral Methods 

Spectral methods approximate the solution of a differential equation using a finite-dimensional space spanned by globally supported basis functions. The basis is chosen to reflect the domain, boundary conditions, and differential operator; standard examples include Fourier modes on pe-riodic domains and orthogonal polynomial systems, such as Chebyshev or Legendre polynomials, on bounded intervals. Galerkin methods impose the equation by projection, whereas collocation and pseudospectral methods evaluate some terms on a quadrature grid. For a periodic Euclidean Schr ¨ odinger equation, the natural specialization is the Fourier pseudospectral method: a finite Fourier series represents the wavefunction, the kinetic operator is diagonal in the Fourier basis, and the potential is evaluated in the dual position representation. This specialization was used for real-space quantum simulation in [Chi+22], and a rigorous end-to-end analysis for black-box Lipschitz potentials was given in [Cha+26]. On a compact Riemannian manifold, the natural generalization is to replace Fourier modes by global eigenfunctions of the nonnegative Laplace–Beltrami operator. One retains a finite spectral subspace, applies the kinetic evolution diagonally in its coefficient basis, and evaluates the poten-tial by mapping to weighted values on a quadrature grid and back. We call this construction the 

global spectral method .15 This formal replacement does not by itself give an efficient digital algorithm. One must con-trol both leakage from the the finite-dimensional space of retained eigenmodes (i.e. Galerkin method) under multiplication by the potential and aliasing when its matrix elements of the pro-jected Hamiltonian are replaced by finite quadrature (i.e. collocation method). In the periodic Euclidean setting, [Cha+26] controls dynamical aliasing for black-box Lipschitz potentials using mollification and high-order Sobolev bounds on the evolving wavefunction. Since the deriva-tive order grows with the dimension and the mollified potential is not band limited, the resulting sufficient grid resolution can be exponential in the dimension. This does not yield a polynomial implementation of the spherical and simplex constructions below, whose transform costs are poly-nomial in the one-dimensional quadrature resolution. For each of the manifolds that we consider, we introduce a uniformly close, exactly band-limited proxy for the potential only in the analysis. Thus for finite time, if the initial wave function has finite bandwidth, then the Galerkin evolution can be shown to be close to a truncate evolu-tion with finite-bandwidth. Since the wavefunction now only have support on a finite band, we can use Gauss quadrature to exactly evaluated the Hamiltonian matrix elements, by sufficient oversampling. The framework below formalizes these analytic and implementation requirements before treating the torus, sphere, and simplex. Consider finite-dimensional spaces spanned by eigenfunctions of the nonnegative Laplace– Beltrami operator −∆g on L2(M). Let {Yα}α∈I be an orthonormal eigenbasis, so that 

−∆gYα = λαYα, (16) where it leave it implicit that we allow for degeneracy, for example λα′ = λα for α̸ = α′. We introduce a binning function for the eigenfunctions L : I → N. Define the ℓ-the level to be

For each of the manifolds we consider, will need to show bound the cost of building this access. In comparison to what [Chi+22; Cha+26], who took Q = N for the Euclidean spectral method, we will choose Q in a different manner. Particularly, our chose of Q will come from analying how the support of a band-limited wavefunction evolves over time given a band-limited potential. The band-limited potential will be a sufficiently close proxy for the true one. Thus a sufficiently large 

Q can make the Galerkin and quadrature-based evolutions equal if both are started from the same initial bounded-bandwidth wavefunction. In what follows, we will prove this first in the abstract setting under a set of sufficient technical conditions. Then in later sections we show that manifolds that we consider satisfy those conditions. The first condition we require the eigenfunction filtration {V N }N≥0 to satisfy what we call slow spectral growth . For subspaces of pointwise-defined functions, write 

VK VN := span {uv : u ∈ V K, v ∈ V N }.We assume that the filtration is invariant under pointwise complex conjugation and that 

VN = VN , VK VN ⊆ V K+N , K, N ∈ N, (27) where the overline denotes pointwise complex conjugation. Equivalently, it is enough to require 

HkHn ⊆ V k+n together with conjugation invariance. Since every level VN is spanned by eigen-functions of −∆g, the kinetic propagator preserves each VN even when a level contains several distinct eigenvalues. In our applications, the relevant levels are Fourier degree ∥k∥∞ on the flat torus, spherical-harmonic degree on Sd, and total Jacobi degree on the simplex. The second requirement is a smoothing operation that, for a bounded function f and target ac-curacy ϵ > 0, produces a band limit K and a function fK ∈ V K such that ∥ f − fK ∥∞ ≤ ϵ. This is to introduce the aformentioend finite-bandwidth proxy for the potential. Then suppose u and 

uK solve the Schr ¨ odinger equations with potentials f and fK, respectively, from a common nor-malized initial state u(0) ∈ V K0 . Write B1(t) := R t 

> 0

∥u(t) − uK (t)∥ ≤ B1(t) ∥ f − fK ∥∞ .The last requirement is a transform condition . For each exactness degree Q, let JQ be the total number of quadrature nodes. Associate to the construction a local resolution parameter RQ ≥ 2satisfying log JQ = O(d log RQ).For a product rule with coordinate node counts RQ,1 , . . . , RQ,d, one may take RQ := max i RQ,i.For every N ≤ Q, we require the weighted evaluation isometry (17) and its adjoint to be imple-mentable to operator error ϵtr using poly (d, N, RQ, log (1/ ϵtr )) 

19 elementary gates and 

O(log JQ + log dim VN + polylog (dNR Q/ϵtr )) 

qubits. For isotropic product grids, RQ = Θ(J1/ dQ ). The torus, sphere, and simplex constructions below satisfy this condition with RQ = Θ(Q).This condition is deliberately weaker than the fast-transform regime available in special cases. Fourier transforms, and Hermite transforms [Jai+26] in settings admitting fast quantum imple-mentations, can have gate complexity polynomial in d, log RQ, and log (1/ ϵtr ). We allow poly-nomial dependence on RQ itself so that the framework also includes the spherical and simplex transforms constructed below, while the required number of qubits remains logarithmic in the total node count. Even in this weaker form, the transform condition is not automatic. For generic polynomial systems on nonrectangular domains, no uniform construction with gate complexity polynomial in d, N, RQ, and log (1/ ϵtr ) is known. The sphere and simplex satisfy the condition because their eigenfunctions admit recursive factorizations into controlled one-dimensional polynomial trans-forms. Furthermore, for our analysis, the transform needs to load the eigenfunctions over a Q-exact quadrature grid (Definition (3.1)) for arbitrary Q. This is critical so that sufficient oversam-pling can make discrete sums equal integrals, i.e. pseudospectral becomes Galerkin. It may be possible to obtain more efficient transforms that load the eigenfunctions over a completely differ-ent grid. With the three main conditions—slow spectral growth, a smoothing operation, and the trans-form condition—in place, we are ready show how these can be used to (1) substantially simplify the analysis of global spectral methods and (2) lead to resource-efficient discretizations. In practice, we will be interested in the N-level Galerkin evolution for the exact potential f ,which we assume accessed is via oracle. As such, the error bound above for truncated fK serves as a useful tool for extracting the true Galerkin error, as given below. The error between the evolu-tions with potential f and fK can be bounded via the previously mentioned Duhamel’s principle argument.

As mentioned earlier, the continuous-space projector PN is replaced with the quadrature-based projection IN,Q given by (20). The discrepancy between IN,Q and PN is referred to as aliasing .More generally, for ψ ∈ V R,

IN,Qψ = PN ψ whenever Q ≥ max (N, R). (42) Indeed, for ⟨Yα, ψ⟩ with L(α) ≤ N, both ψ and Yα belong to VQ, so the identity follows directly from Q-exactness of the discrete inner product and (20). This has the immediate implication. 

Proposition 3.5 (Exact quadrature implementation of the Galerkin projector) . Suppose the filtration 

{V N }N≥0 satisfies slow spectral growth, f K ∈ V K, and I N,Q is constructed from a Q-exact quadrature rule. If Q ≥ N + K, then IN,Q VN+K = PN VN+K . (43) 

In particular, for every v ∈ V N ,IN,Q( fK v) = PN ( fK v). (44) 

Proof. Equation (43) follows from (42). Slow spectral growth gives fK v ∈ V N+K, which proves (44). We now use this exactness to pass from the continuum Galerkin estimate to the quadrature evolution.

3.2 Flat Torus 

The flat torus is the Riemannian quotient manifold Td = Rd/Zd with Euclidean metric. Con-cretely, it admits a global parametrization via the set S = [ −1/2, 1/2 ]d, with periodic boundary conditions in each coordinate. For the torus, the Laplace–Beltrami operator ∆g is diagonalized by Fourier modes and there already exists other simulation algorithms for the torus [Chi+22; Cha+26]. We present an alternative algorithm and analysis that leads to exponential improvement in discretization spacing and a quadratic reduction in the number of qubits in terms of d. Through a change-of-coordinates, this also leads to simulation algorithms for the positive orthant with the log-barrier Hessian metric, polyhedral log-barrier Hessian metric for full-rank constraint matrix, and ℓp balls. We start by presenting a smoothing procedure that takes f ∈ L2(M) and maps it to a finite-degree Fourier polynomial with controllable error. For an integer m ≥ 2, define the following Jackson Kernel

Apply Corollary 3.6 with f = g, fK = gK, and the observable proxy hK. Its observable estimate gives 

⟨Ψ(T)|bhGQ |Ψ(T)⟩ − ⟨ Φ(T)|Mh|Φ(T)⟩

≤ 4B1 ∥hK ∥∞ ∥g − gK ∥∞ + 23−q ∥hK ∥∞ + 2 ∥h − hK ∥∞

≤ 2 ∥h∥∞ ϵ + ∥h∥∞ ϵ + ∥h∥∞ ϵ = 4 ∥h∥∞ ϵ.Theorem 3.8 should be compared with Theorem 4.8 and Lemma 4.7 of [Cha+26], which to-gether provide the discretization guarantees used by its main simulation theorem [Cha+26, The-orem 4.2]. The first of those results proves L2-closeness between the Fourier interpolant of the collocation evolution and the continuum solution. Because the collocation grid also fixes the re-tained Fourier band, its proof controls dynamical aliasing through high-order Sobolev norms of the evolving wavefunction. Bounding those norms for a Lipschitz potential requires mollification and a Sobolev-growth estimate, and leads in general to a grid resolution that is exponential in the dimension. Here the Jackson proxy is instead exactly band limited, and oversampling makes its quadrature potential identical to the Galerkin potential. The state error is therefore controlled only by the uniform proxy error and the tail of the Dyson series, with no Sobolev-norm estimate for the evolved wavefunction, yielding a grid resolution polynomial in the dimension under the assumptions of the theorem. The expectation-value comparison is simplified for the same reason. The general expectation-error lemma of [Cha+26] contains, in addition to the state error, a Riemann-summation error in-volving sup ∇| ΦM|2 

> ∞

and a Fourier-tail term involving |Φ|H1 /M. Obtaining an end-to-end guarantee requires bounding these wavefunction-dependent quantities, and the generic estimate sup ∇| ΦM|2 

> ∞

= O(Md+1) contributes to the large grid requirement. In the present anal-ysis, the observable proxy hK is band limited and the oversampled rule integrates hK | eΨ|2 ex-actly The observable error therefore reduces to the state error and the uniform approximation error ∥h − hK ∥∞; no separate Riemann-summation or density-gradient estimate is needed. The Hamiltonian-simulation primitive, and hence its oracle-query bound, may still be taken from [Cha+26, Theorem 4.4]; the improvement is in the discretization and qubit requirements. For concreteness, we present an improved version of Theorem [Cha+26, Theorem 4.2] using our new discretization result. The torus case is equivalent to their periodic boundary conditions case. The case without periodic boundary conditions can be handlded using the same δ low-leakage evolution assumption [Cha+26, Definition 4.5].

The rescaled evolution lasts for τg = Λg B1 and has a norm-one interaction Hamiltonian. On each sign interval of b, a time-controlled interaction-picture query consists of the compressed-potential block surrounded by the controlled kinetic phases; intervals on which b = 0 are omitted. The access assumptions supply the sign of b, the inverse time conversion, and the controlled phases. The rescaled truncated-Dyson interaction-picture algorithm [LW18; Ber+20] simulates this evo-lution to operator error O(ϵ), with failure probability O(ϵ), using the number of time-controlled queries in (77). Returning to the Schr ¨ odinger picture requires one final kinetic phase. Each interaction-picture query contains one sampled-potential block and hence two binary-oracle calls, one forward padded QFT, one inverse padded QFT, and two controlled kinetic phases. This proves (77). Regard the synthesized transform sandwich as an exact block encoding of a nearby com-pressed Hamiltonian. Duhamel’s formula contributes B1ϵg + O(τgδ) from this Hamiltonian per-turbation, while the hybrid argument over the time-controlled queries contributes another O(ϵ)

from kinetic-phase and schedule synthesis. By the choice of δ, the truncated-Dyson, oracle, trans-form, kinetic, and schedule errors therefore sum to O(ϵ). Initial-state preparation adds at most

3.3 Positive orthant (and its invertible affine images) with log-barrier metrics 

Here we will consider the setting of simulating the Schr ¨ odinger equation constrained a d-dimensional polyhedron P defined a by Ax ≤ b, where A is invertible. The associated metric with be the Hes-sian metric induced by the log-barrier function

∇2Φ(x).The operator on the full orthant has continuous spectrum, and an infinite domain cannot be represented by a finite digital register. We consider a truncated orthant for two reasons: (1) the above does not have a point spectrum and (2) because we cannot digitally encode the infinite 33 orthant. We therefore restrict to [r−, r+]d, where 0 < r− < r+ < ∞. A self-adjoint realization on this compact box remains unbounded but has compact resolvent. 3.4 ℓp balls with a Duffy metric 

We now consider simulation on ℓp balls. The cases p = 1 and p = 2 have familiar geometric descriptions: each orthant of the ℓ1 ball is a simplex, while the ℓ2 sphere carries the standard round metric. For p /∈ { 1, 2 }, there is no comparably distinguished metric adapted to the ℓp geometry with a known tractable eigenbasis. The metric inherited from the ambient Euclidean space is one possible choice, but the corresponding eigenfunctions are not known in closed form in general. We instead choose a metric that becomes Euclidean under the Duffy map. Schr ¨ odinger evolution on the resulting compact subsets of an ℓp ball and the upper half of an ℓp sphere then reduces to the now familiar evolution on the flat torus. Let p ≥ 2. This assumption makes the maps below at least C2 on every compact coordinate box used in the construction, which provides the regularity needed for the Laplace–Beltrami operators below. The open ℓp ball and the upper hemisphere of the ℓp sphere are

For the transform-complexity statements below, the quadrature nodes, weights, normalized polynomial values, and orthogonal completions are supplied by exact-real classical preprocess-ing. The stated transform error therefore refers to quantum circuit synthesis. We also require the following classical preprocessing to construct the bAj,h maps. For each pair (j, h) with 2 ≤ j ≤ d

and 0 ≤ h ≤ N, perform the following one-time classical preprocessing for the declared values of 

(d, N, Q, ϵtr ).1. Form the R × R tridiagonal Jacobi matrix for the fixed base weight (1 − z2)(j−2)/2 . Diagonal-ize it to obtain the common nodes zj,r and positive weights wj,r.2. Evaluate the normalized factors ϕ(j)

> n,h

(zj,r ) for 0 ≤ n ≤ N − h. Stable three-term evaluation uses (160); normalization uses (162). Assemble the active matrix Wj,h in (158). 3. Verify, to the working classical precision, that A† 

> j,h

Aj,h = I. Complete its columns determin-istically to an R × R orthogonal matrix eAj,h, for example by a QR completion seeded by the unused computational basis vectors. 4. Apply QR elimination to eAj,h and record at most R(R − 1)/2 Givens rotations, each specified by two row indices and one angle, together with the residual diagonal signs. Round every angle to O(log (d(N + 1)R2/ϵtr )) bits. 5. Store the node and weight tables for each j, and store or provide a uniform classical circuit that emits the Givens list controlled on (j, h). The latter is required for a uniform quantum circuit family. Tridiagonal eigensolution costs O(R2) per level, factor evaluation costs O(NR ) per controlled block, and dense completion plus Givens elimination costs O(R3) per controlled block. Hence the conservative total is O(d(N + 1)R3) arithmetic operations and O(d(N + 1)R2) stored rotation data. The centered azimuthal transform is the QFT over ZMφ under signed labels. Since Mφ =

2Q + 1 need not be a power of two, its logical register is embedded in ⌈log 2 Mφ⌉ qubits and the transform is extended unitarily on unused basis states. An arbitrary-modulus QFT compiled to error ϵtr /2 uses poly (log Mφ, log (1/ ϵtr )) elementary gates, which is included in the transform-error budget below. To evaluate a function on Sd in rectangular coordinates, we can use the following mapping (Duffy map) from the rectangular grid to spherical grid: reversibly evaluate the associated node coordinates and product weight when a Cartesian point oracle or a potential-value oracle requires them. One important qualification is that Algorithm 1 is not quantumly efficient in the usual sense. Specifically, the dependence on the number of grid points in a single radial coordinate R is not logarithmic. However, the discretization theorem keeps R polynomial in the stated problem pa-rameters and inverse accuracy; in particular, R = poly (d, 1/ ϵ) when the remaining parameters are held fixed.

Standard ancilla-free de-compositions give a quadratic polylogarithmic overhead instead and do not change the stated asymptotic bound. QR elimination reduces one R × R orthogonal matrix using at most R(R − 1)/2 such rotations and leaves a diagonal matrix D = diag (±1). Reconstructing the matrix uses the inverse rotations in reverse elimination order, followed by the recorded sign correction. Each negative entry of 

D is an addressed phase flip and has the same polylogarithmic binary-address cost; all O(R)

possible sign corrections are lower order than the O(R2) rotations. This also covers an orthogonal completion of determinant −1, which cannot be represented by determinant-one Givens rotations alone. There are d − 1 angular levels and at most N + 1 control values h per level. Each padded 

R-dimensional unitary uses O(R2) Givens rotations. Multiplying the three counts gives

The rescaled evolution lasts for τf = Λ f B1 and has a norm-one interaction Hamiltonian. On each sign interval of b, a time-controlled interaction-picture query consists of the compressed-potential block surrounded by the controlled kinetic phases; intervals on which b = 0 are omitted. The access assumptions supply the sign of b, the inverse time conversion, and the controlled phases. The rescaled truncated-Dyson interaction-picture algorithm [LW18; Ber+20] simulates this evo-lution to operator error O(ϵ), with failure probability O(ϵ), using the number of time-controlled queries in (187). Returning to the Schr ¨ odinger picture requires one final kinetic phase. Each 56 interaction-picture query contains one sampled-potential block and hence two binary-oracle calls, one forward spherical transform, one inverse spherical transform, and two controlled kinetic phases. This proves (187). Regard the synthesized transform sandwich as an exact block encoding of a nearby com-pressed Hamiltonian. Duhamel’s formula contributes B1ϵ f + O(τf δ) from this Hamiltonian per-turbation, while the hybrid argument over the time-controlled queries contributes another O(ϵ)

Lemma 3.20. Let Poly ≤K (Sd) be the space of polynomials on the simplex of degree at most K. Then for all K, 

VK = Poly ≤K (Sd).

Proof. Consider the multivariable Jacobi polynomial in barycentric coordinates (193). A single one-variable Jacobi polynomial factor is a polynomial of degree ni in xi/si. Consequently, each bracket in (193) is a sum of terms 

xqi sni −qi , 0 ≤ q ≤ ni, (196) and is a barycentric polynomial of degree ni. Their product has total degree at most |n|. In partic-ular, 

∥n∥1 ≤ K =⇒ Pn ∈ Poly ≤K (Sd).Taking spans gives the first, nontrivial inclusion Jac ≤K (Sd) ⊆ Poly ≤K (Sd).Conversely, the nonzero polynomials {P n : ∥n∥ ≤ K} are mutually orthogonal in L2(Sd, dvol g)

and hence linearly independent. Note that #{n ∈ Nd : ∥n∥1 ≤ K} =

K + dd



= dim Poly ≤K (Sd).Hence Poly ≤K (Sd) = Jac ≤K (Sd).The above makes it apparent that the Jacobi polynomials satisfy the slow expansion property. # 4 Simulation on General Riemannian Manifolds – Coherent Chart Spec-tral Methods 

This section develops a more generic simulation algorithm for Reimannian Schr ¨ odinger equations by applying local Euclidean spectral methods in a finite atlas and coherently recombining the local solutions. These models require an extended access model that provides more information than what was required by the global spectral methods. This so we can properly localize the global wavefunction on each coordinate chart and apply the local spectral method. Critical to this is the notion of subordinate partition of unity. The collection of smooth, compactly supported, real-valued functions on some open set U ⊆

M is denoted C∞ 

> c

(U).

Definition 4.1 (Partitions of Unity) . We say a collection {χα ∈ C∞ 

> c

(M)}α∈I is a partition of unity if 

# ∑

> α∈I

χ2

> α

(x) = 1, ∀x ∈ M . (224) Taking the square in Eq. (224) is nonstandard, but is convenient for L2 spaces. A partition of unity 

{χα}, is subordinate to an atlas A = {(Uℓ, κℓ)}ℓ∈[nch ] if the support of each partition χα is contained in some Uℓ. For a PoU subordinate to a finite atlas, one can always define a new finite PoU {χℓ}

indexed with the chart. When we speak of subordinate PoUs we will always assume this finitely-indexed version. For a function ψ ∈ L2(M), the L2 norm can be expressed in terms of the subordinate partition of unity as 

∥ψ∥2 =

for every ℓ and the smaller coordinate neighborhoods {κ−1

Definition 4.2 (Box-subordinate PoU) . Let A be a box-adapted atlas. A real quadratic PoU {χℓ}ℓ∈I is

The support of each bump function being a positive distance from the bound of S ensure a chart-dependent collar of ∂S on which every localized representative (χℓψ) ◦ κ−1

In particular, N = poly (d) for fixed target accuracy. Proof. The first term in Theorem 4.9 decays as N−(s−2), while the second term decays faster as 

N−s. Taking the (s − 2)th root of the assumed prefactor gives a polynomial in d when s = Θ(d),which proves (285). The reason that we considered the continuous model is that discretization introduces an addi-tional obstacle: overlapping charts need not have intersecting local grids. A global source inter-polant transfers one coefficient representation to all local grids. For a general high-dimensional manifold this map may be inefficient; the factorized construction below is conditional on constant-dimensional product structure and explicit implementation assumptions. 78 4.2 Tensor products of manifolds and a discretized algorithm 

This subsection presents a discretized version of the preceding algorithm for tensor products of constant-dimensional manifolds. The low-order geometric parameters of the factors are assumed to be bounded uniformly in the number of factors. Under the approximation assumptions stated below, the local Fourier cutoff is polynomially bounded for fixed target accuracy. This cutoff theorem is not, by itself, a polynomial-runtime theorem: such a result additionally requires poly-nomial bounds on the source-space dimensions, block-encoding costs, oracle costs, and integrated Hamiltonian normalizations. A global source interpolant resolves this mismatch. For a general high-dimensional manifold that interpolation may be computationally expensive; however for (286), it can be assembled coherently from constant-dimensional factor interpolants. We do not select a particular global interpolation basis. However, one such option is com-pactly supported wavelets. The result instead assumes stated approximation and implementation properties of the source spaces and derives a cutoff bound contingent on those properties. The first part of this section will state the algorithm, while the second part is dedicated to the error analysis. 79 4.2.1 Digital Coherent-Charts Method 

The aforementioned global interpolation basis defines a Hilbert space of achievable wavefunctions that globally approximate the true wave function via an interpolation from a fix set of points on the manifold. Each component ν is given a separate interpolating basis, whose span is referred to as the source space .For each ν Choose an L2(Mν)-orthonormal basis Yν := {L(ν)

Thus the interpolating basis for the overall manifold M is just the tensor product of the component global interpolating bases. Each source space is associated with a parameter Rν that we call the resolution parameter . Under the explicit block-encoding and oracle-access assumptions above, the algorithm need not materialize an Nd × Nd resampling matrix or loop over all product nodes: it applies m

constant-dimensional resampling and kinetic primitives coherently to the factor registers. This conclusion depends on those access assumptions and does not follow from register factorization alone. The same formal construction applies beyond product manifolds, but a global interpolation may then be inefficient. Showing a complete polynomial gate complexity still hinges on the effi-ciency of block encoding A, as even those each component manifold is constant dimensional, the number of global interpolation functions and grid points may grow with d. Still, we make a stride in this direction by analyzing a setting where the discretization parameter Nν can be taken to be poly (m).

4.2.2 Discretization error analysis for products of constant dimensional manifolds 

The digital coherent-chart error on the product manifold (286) is analyzed by comparing the exact and fully discrete evolutions in four stages: projection onto the source space, approximation of the geometric coefficients, approximation on the local grids, and comparison with the final discrete operator. Duhamel’s formula then combines the four errors together. The main result, Theorem 4.23, assumes that the dimensions, numbers of charts, chart distor-tions, and the metric, density, cutoff, and IMS quantities of the factors are bounded independently 83 of the factor and of d. It also assumes that one common source resolution can be chosen for all factors, at least as large as N divided by a polynomial in d. Along this choice, the source-space projection error defined in (315) and the error from representing a source-space function on a local grid defined in (388) must decay at the rates stated in the theorem. Finally, the derivatives of the geometric coefficients and the Sobolev norms used in the later estimates are allowed to grow with 

d, but by no more than (Cd β)d; their precise definitions appear in (345), (420), and Theorem 4.23. Under these assumptions, each of the four errors is at most (Cd β/N)d. Thus a resolution poly-nomial in d suffices for fixed target accuracy. No particular construction of the source spaces is required. For clarity, use common resolutions Rν = R and Nν = N for every factor. Accordingly, write 

Vsrc  

> R

, Qsrc  

> R

, ER, Ksrc  

> R,N

, Msrc   

> f,R,N

, and Hsrc  

> R,N

for the objects previously defined with R = ( R, . . . , R) and 

N = ( N, . . . , N).The local Fourier construction does not prescribe the global source spaces, so their projection errors are retained explicitly below. We use the following notation for the factorwise Sobolev norms. Proposition 4.17. If Npad ≥ N + 3κJ, (385) 

then BJ,pad  

> ν,N

= BG 

> ν,N

. (386) When nJ = N, one has κJ = 2(N − 1), so the condition above becomes Npad ≥ N + 6(N − 1).

Proof. An input in the truncated Fourier space has support in [−N, N − 1]pν . Reading the kinetic product from right to left, the two h multipliers and the Cij multiplier enlarge the final kinetic support to 

[−N − 3κJ, N − 1 + 3κJ]pν ; (387) the intermediate supports use one and two copies of κJ in the same way. Fourier differentiation does not enlarge support, and the IMS branch lies in [−N − κJ, N − 1 + κJ]pν . The padded retained set is [−Npad , Npad − 1]pν , so both endpoints fit under exactly the same condition (385), no inter-mediate crosses the padded frequency boundary. Circular convolution on the padded grid thus equals ordinary Fourier convolution at every multiplication. The final adjoint zero-padding map performs the left Galerkin projection, proving (386). Thus padding changes the result in Lemma 4.16 through N → Npad ; in particular, the two differentiation matrices replace the factor N2 by N2pad . In particular, take nJ = N and the minimal padding L = Npad = N + 6(N − 1) ≤ 7N. The two differentiation matrices cost exactly the factor 

O(N2) that converts the coefficient approximation rate N−(d+2) into the required padded-matrix rate N−d.Padding removes coefficient-multiplication aliasing but not all aliasing in the method. The localization map T multiplies by a function of infinite bandwidth, and the source-space localiza-tion coefficients are themselves computed from grid samples. These effects are controlled jointly through the defect

(R, N) := sup

103 Proof. Choose the Jackson order nJ = N. Since its coefficient bandwidth is κJ = 2(N − 1), choose the minimal de-aliasing padding 

Npad = N + 3κJ = N + 6(N − 1) ≤ 7N.These choices satisfy the padding condition in Proposition 4.17 and do not change the asymptotic resolution. The uniform factor hypothesis and pν ≤ p0 imply sup 

> 1≤ν≤m

Cgeom   

> T,ν,2

= O(1).The low-order envelopes Gν,0 , Gν,1 , and Gν,2 are also uniformly bounded. Inspecting their defini-tions in the preceding consistency lemmas therefore gives sup

> 1≤ν≤m

AFourier, J = O(1).Finally, Npad ≤ 7N and the displayed formula for Ad,J give sup 

> 1≤ν≤m

Ad,J

N2 = O(1).These conclusions use only uniform low-order factor data. They do not control the high-order envelope Gν,d+2. They also do not control the comparison-evolution norms. Those quantities are assumed separately above. The lower bound on R(N) gives 

R(N)−d ≤ c−dR dγR d N−d.Therefore the two source terms of order R−d, as well as the faster R−(d+2) terms, are bounded by 

(C1dβ1 /N)d after enlarging constants independent of d and N. Hence Esrc (T, R) has this form. Since nJ = N, the continuum Jackson factor is N−d. The coefficient and regularity assumptions, together with the sum over at most m ≤ d factors, therefore give the same bound for EJ,cont (T, R).For the local Fourier term, ηsrc  

> ν,2

= O(N−d) and ηsrc  

> ν,0

5.1 Simulations of Physics 

The preceding sections develop quantum algorithms for Hamiltonians of the form ˆH(t) = −a(t)∆G + b(t)V, (436) acting on L2(M, d vol G ), where (M, G) is a Riemannian configuration space and V : M → R

is a real potential. Here, V corresponds to the potential f used in the preceding sections. In this section, we use G for the metric on the full physical configuration space and reserve g for the metric on the target space associated with a single site or link. This section explains why Hamiltonians of the form (436) arise naturally in physics. For a con-strained quantum-mechanical system, as discussed in Section 5.1.1, M is a curved configuration space. For a spatially discretized quantum field theory, it is often a Cartesian product M = T|Λ|

of copies of a fixed-dimensional target manifold T. The product metric makes the kinetic operator a sum of Laplace–Beltrami operators on the factors, while spatial-gradient, on-site, and plaquette terms contribute to the potential V.

5.1.1 Quantum Mechanics on Curved Spaces 

The most immediate case is the one that requires no construction at all. A single non-relativistic particle moving on a Riemannian manifold (M, G) under a potential V : M → R has the Hamil-tonian ˆH = − ¯h2

2m ∆G + V (x), x ∈ M , (437) acting on L2(M), where ∆G is the Laplace–Beltrami operator of G and m is the mass. This is already the form assumed in the beginning of this section, requiring neither a lattice regularization nor any field-theoretic input. It is also the template of which the remaining sections are instances: each arrives at (437) on a configuration space that is a Cartesian product, through its own choice of factor manifold, metric and potential. Such Hamiltonians arise whenever a mechanical system is subject to constraints, so that its configuration space is a curved manifold rather than a Euclidean space. The quantization of con-strained systems, and the operator-ordering question it raises, are discussed by DeWitt [DeW57]. Quantum systems governed by Hamiltonians of the form (437) arise in several concrete set-tings. These include particles constrained to prescribed geometries [Cos81b], with geometry-induced potentials in quantum nanostructures providing a prominent example [SBR17], as well as molecular Hamiltonians [Mey02] and shell-shaped atomic gases [TS24]. Treatments of the lat-ter two settings place particular emphasis on motion on the sphere S2. Moreover, manifolds with nontrivial topology may support topological terms, providing physically interesting toy models for more complicated systems. A particle on a circle with such a term has, for example, been studied as a target for quantum simulation in [She+22]. Although our present algorithm does not include topological terms, this example suggests a natural direction in which the framework could be extended. Quantum field theory on a curved spacetime is a different setting, since there the metric is a metric on spacetime rather than on the configuration space of the dynamical variables, and the 105 configuration space remains a space of field configurations. We therefore do not consider it in these notes. The models below may be field theories, but only on a flat Minkowski spacetime. 

5.1.2 Nonlinear Sigma Models 

Nonlinear sigma models (NLSMs) are an important prototypical class of models in physics. They were first introduced in [GL60] to describe pions and now play a broad role in both high-energy and condensed matter physics (see the review [Bur00] for a general introduction). A promi-nent particle-physics example is chiral perturbation theory, whose leading-order Lagrangian is that of a nonlinear sigma model with target manifold SU (Nf ) [Eck+89]. In condensed matter physics, the O(3) nonlinear sigma model, whose target manifold is S2, describes the low-energy behavior of Heisenberg antiferromagnets in one spatial dimension [Hal83] and two spatial dimen-sions [CHN89]. Reflecting this broad relevance, the O(N) models have also attracted considerable interest as targets for quantum simulation [SM05; Ale+19; SC19; Sin22]. Their target manifold is the sphere SN−1, with the corresponding O(N) symmetry. The Lagrangian (density) of the Nonlinear Sigma Model for a target manifold T of dimension 

d(T) on a (D + 1)-dimensional Minkowski spacetime is

The target manifold T is equipped with a Riemannian metric g, the field is a map Σ : R1, D → T from spacetime into the target, and Σa (a = 1, . . . , d(T)) denotes the components of Σ in a local chart of 

T. Correspondingly, gab (Σ(x)) is the metric of T evaluated at the field value Σ(x). The potential 

V : T → R is a fixed function on the target. It is worth emphasizing the change of viewpoint relative to the main analysis of the paper. There, the dynamical variable is a point “ x” of a manifold. Here, the dynamical variable is the field value Σ, taking values in the manifold T, while the spacetime coordinate x merely labels which copy of Σ we are talking about. This will become concrete in Section 5.1.2, where discretizing space turns the label x into a lattice site and the configuration space into a Cartesian product of copies of T, one per site.
