5.1.3 CPN−1 Models 

The CPN−1 models [Eic78; dLD78] are nonlinear sigma models whose target is complex projective space. In two spacetime dimensions, they are associated with asymptotic freedom and confine-ment, and possess instantons and a θ-vacuum structure. They are for this reason a long-standing toy model for the corresponding phenomena in four-dimensional gauge theory [Wit79]. From our point of view the essential point is that a CPN−1 model is an instance of Section 5.1.2 with a different choice of (T, g). We therefore only need to identify the target manifold and its met-ric. The Legendre transform, lattice regularization and quantization of Section 5.1.2 then naturally apply to the case of CPN−1.

5.1.4 Lattice Gauge Theories 

Lattice gauge theories [Wil74] (LGTs) are the standard non-perturbative regularization of gauge theory, and the Hamiltonian formulation of Kogut and Susskind [KS75] is the natural starting point for quantum simulation [Ba ˜ n+20]. We keep the gauge group G an arbitrary compact con-nected Lie group, so that G = SU (3) recovers lattice QCD and G = U(1) compact lattice electro-dynamics. On a fixed lattice, the Kogut–Susskind Hamiltonian [KS75] is already of the form −∆G + V

on a Cartesian product of copies of a fixed compact manifold, with no further continuum limit needed to bring it there. The target manifold is the gauge group G itself, so its dimension is fixed by the theory and does not grow with the lattice. The one structural difference from Section 5.1.2 is that the degrees of freedom live on the links of the lattice rather than on its sites. We restrict discussions throughout to the gauge degrees of freedom, so that what we describe is more precisely a lattice Yang–Mills theory. This pure-gauge sector already carries much of the essential non-perturbative physics. Moreover, a complete theory such as lattice QCD adds matter fields on the sites, but retains the pure-gauge Hamiltonian below as a summand. Throughout we fix once and for all a faithful unitary representation of G and identify group elements with the corresponding unitary matrices, so that Tr and † below are the ordinary matrix trace and conjugate transpose. 

The configuration space Let Λ = ( aZ/LZ)D be a spatial lattice as in Section 5.1.2, and let

This is the announced form −∆G + V on a Cartesian product of copies of the single fixed compact manifold G.111 5.1.5 Concrete simulation of the spatially discretized O(N) nonlinear sigma model 

We now give a concrete application of the sphere algorithm to a particular lattice realization of the nonlinear sigma model. Let O(N) be the orthogonal group of real N × N matrices that preserve the Euclidean inner product on RN . An O(N) field takes values in the unit sphere SN−1 ⊂ RN ,and the group acts on every field value by the same orthogonal transformation. The round metric and any interaction formed from inner products of field values are invariant under this action. Fix N ≥ 3, let Λ = ( aZ/LZ)D be a finite periodic spatial lattice, and write nΛ := |Λ|. Afield configuration is a tuple σ = ( σn)n∈Λ ∈ (SN−1)nΛ . We use the squared chordal distance in the ambient Euclidean space. Each coordinate function σa on SN−1 is a degree-one spherical harmonic, so 1 − σ · σ′ contains only degrees zero and one in each spin variable. Multiplication by one nearest-neighbor interaction therefore raises the spherical-harmonic degree at either endpoint by at most one. The interaction depends only on the inner product and hence preserves the O(N)

symmetry. Since 12 ∥σ − σ′∥22 = 1 − σ · σ′ on the unit sphere, the resulting Hamiltonian is

> J

. The tensor product of the single-site quadrature rules is Q-exact for the product filtration. Since Q = J + 1, Proposition 3.5 identifies the quadrature compression of 

Vch with its Galerkin compression. Corollary 3.6, with fK = f = Vch and B1 = T, bounds the discretization error by 2 2−q ≤ ϵ.The product transform is implemented by applying the compiled single-site transform at ev-ery lattice site. Synthesizing each factor to operator error δ/nΛ makes the product-transform error at most δ. The kinetic phase is computed from the sum of the single-site eigenvalues ℓ(ℓ + N − 2),and the sampled potential is computed from the nearest-neighbor inner products. The interaction-picture proof of Theorem 3.19 therefore applies with one forward and one inverse product trans-form in each potential query. A product transform invokes nΛ single-site transforms, each with sphere dimension N − 1 and spectral cutoff J, so its gate cost is eO(nΛ(N − 1)( J + 1)( Q + 1)2).Substituting this cost, the potential-evaluation cost Cch , and the normalization Λch into (187)–(189) proves (476)–(479). 113 5.2 Connection to quantum interior-point methods 

Let X ⊆ Rd be a convex set with nonempty interior, and consider the convex optimization prob-lem min  

> x∈X

⟨c, x⟩. (480) Given a ϑ-self-concordant barrier Φ : int (X ) → R, path-following interior-point methods con-sider the barrier objectives 

Vη (x) := η⟨c, x⟩ + Φ(x), x(η) := arg min  

> x∈int (X)

Vη (x). (481) As η increases, the central path {x(η)}η>0 approaches the optimal set of (480). The Hessian of the barrier defines the local metric gΦ = ∇2Φ. Since the objective is linear, it is also the Hessian of Vη ,and the Newton step at an interior point x is 

−∇ 2Φ(x)−1 ηc + ∇Φ(x). (482) Thus the same local geometry determines the Newton direction, the Dikin ellipsoid in which a step remains feasible, and the notion of proximity used to follow the central path. Self-concordance controls the variation of this metric between nearby points and is the central regularity property in the classical path-following analysis [NN88; NN94; Ren01]. Gribling, Apers, Nieuwboer, and Walter proposed a quantum analogue of this path-following picture [Gri+25]. They associate to each value of η the Riemannian Schr ¨ odinger operator 

HR(η) := − 12 ∆Φ + γ2Vη , (483) where ∆Φ is the Laplace–Beltrami operator of gΦ = ∇2Φ and γ is a semiclassical parameter. In the barrier metric, the harmonic approximation near x(η) is normalized, rather than inheriting the Euclidean condition number of ∇2Φ(x(η)) . Their analysis makes this intuition quantitative: 

Theorem 5.2 ([Gri+25, Theorem 1.2]) . There is a universal constant C > 0 such that, for d ≥ 2 and 

γ ≥ C(d log d)5, the operators in (483) satisfy, for every η ≥ 0,

λ1(HR(η)) − λ0(HR(η)) ≥ γ

2 . (484) In particular, this lower bound is uniform along the central path and has no dependence on a Euclidean Hessian condition number. Early quantum interior-point methods used quantum linear-system algorithms to approximate the Newton direction at each iteration, and their run-times consequently depended polynomially on a uniform upper bound for the condition numbers of the Newton systems encountered along the central path [Aug+23b; KP18]. Such a bound may be large and must hold over the entire run. By incorporating the barrier Hessian into the kinetic geometry, the Riemannian construction removes this particular dependence from the spectral-gap estimate underlying quantum path following. For sufficiently large γ, the corresponding ground state is concentrated near x(η). The cited work uses these states to define a discrete quantum central path: under assumptions including an initial state with sufficient ground-state overlap, estimates of the ground-state energies, and access to controlled Riemannian Hamiltonian evolu-tion, rotations about a sequence of neighboring ground states prepare a state concentrated near an approximate optimizer. 114 The simulation results of this paper address one of the primitives required by that framework. For the standard logarithmic barrier Φ(x) = − ∑di=1 log xi on the positive orthant, Theorem 3.11 gives a digital discretization of the evolution generated by −∆Φ plus a bounded Lipschitz poten-tial after restricting to a box [a, b]d and imposing Dirichlet boundary conditions. For any fixed η,the restriction of Vη to this box is such a potential, so the theorem applies to a compactly truncated version of HR(η), subject to its stated initial-state and regularity hypotheses. This provides a rig-orous finite-dimensional simulation result in a canonical barrier geometry, but it does not by itself instantiate the complete quantum path-following algorithm. Several additional steps would be needed to instantiate an end-to-end algorithm. In partic-ular, one must choose a truncation that works uniformly over the relevant central-path param-eters, control the effect of its artificial boundary on ground-state concentration, spectral gaps, and neighboring-state overlaps, and account for controlled simulation, ground-energy estima-tion, state preparation, and readout. The orthant result should be viewed as a digital simulation primitive whose integration into a fully discrete quantum interior-point method remains an open problem. 

5.3 Riemannian Quantum Hamiltonian Descent 

In this section, we extend Quantum Hamiltonian Descent [Len+23] to non-Euclidean geometry. Abe and Nagai recently proposed Riemannian Quantum Hamiltonian Descent at the operator and path-integral levels, derived semiclassical equations of motion, and numerically studied rep-resentative examples [AN26]. They also gave a conditional oracle-level simulation analysis, as-suming phase-oracle access to the objective and sparse access to a chosen discretization of the Laplace–Beltrami operator. Complementing this formulation, we develop rigorous convergence guarantees for geodesically convex objectives, including the relevant boundary terms, and com-bine them with explicit digital simulation algorithms on the sphere and the Fisher–Rao simplex. These results make the Riemannian QHD algorithm concrete in settings where the required geo-metric Hamiltonians can be implemented and analyzed explicitly. 

5.3.1 Additional Preliminaries 

We record the differential-geometric identities used in the computation relevant to this subsection. Here we note ∇V ψ = V(ψ) for vector field V. Metric compatibility of the Levi–Civita connection gives the covariant product rule 

X ⟨Y, Z⟩g = ⟨∇ XY, Z⟩g + ⟨Y, ∇X Z⟩g . (485) For a smooth scalar function u, its Hessian is the covariant 2-tensor Hess u(X, Y) = ⟨∇ X ∇u, Y⟩g = X(Yu ) − (∇XY)u, (486) and ∆gu is its metric trace. The first equality means that the Hessian and the covariant derivative of the gradient contain the same information: 

∇X ∇u = ( Hess u(X, ·)) ♯. (487) Here, Hess u(X, ·) is the 1-form obtained by fixing the first slot and ♯ converts that 1-form to a vector using the metric. Thus the Hessian is the Riemannian analogue of the Jacobian matrix of the vector field ∇u.115 The divergence of a covariant 2-tensor S is the 1-form

Interchanging those indices produces the Ricci curvature term derived below. In flat Euclidean space all covari-ant derivatives are ordinary partial derivatives, so the indices commute and the two expressions agree. The frame removes connection coefficients at q, but not their derivatives; the latter are what produce curvature when third covariant derivatives are commuted. With the curvature convention, we obtain the Ricci curvature tensor R:

R(X, Y)Z = ∇X ∇Y Z − ∇ Y ∇X Z − ∇ [X,Y] Z, (494) 116 the vector R(X, Y)Z measures the failure of covariant derivatives of the vector field Z to commute in the directions X and Y. Specifically, it follows that 

(∇X Hess u)( Y, Z) − (∇Y Hess u)( X, Z) = ⟨R(X, Y)∇u, Z⟩g . (495) The Riemannian QHD convergence proof relies heavily on the following lemmas. For sym-metric differential expressions X and Y on a common smooth core, set

5.3.2 Riemannian QHD over the Sphere 

Let M = Sd be the unit round sphere. Fix x⋆ ∈ Sd and let 

r(x) = dSd (x, x⋆), ϕ(x) = 12 r(x)2.Assume 

Ω ⊂ BSd (x⋆, R), 0 < R < π,and that Ω is geodesically convex with smooth boundary. Away from x⋆, the spherical metric in geodesic polar coordinates is 

g = dr2 + sin 2 r g Sd−1 .For a radial function h(r),Hess (h) = h′′ (r) dr ⊗ dr + h′(r) cot r (g − dr ⊗ dr). (583) 128 Taking h(r) = 12 r2, so h′(r) = r and h′′ (r) = 1, gives Hess (ϕ) = dr ⊗ dr + r cot r (g − dr ⊗ dr). (584) For 0 < r < π, one has r cot r ≤ 1. Therefore Hess (ϕ) ⪯ g on BSd (x⋆, R). (585) At r = 0, the same conclusion follows by smooth extension. If Ω is geodesically convex and x⋆ ∈ Ω, then the outward star-shaped condition also holds: 

∂nϕ = ⟨∇ ϕ, n⟩g ≥ 0 on ∂Ω. (586) Indeed, the minimizing geodesic from a boundary point x to x⋆ points inward, so − log x (x⋆) = 

∇ϕ(x) has nonnegative pairing with the outward normal. For a radial function h(r) on Sd,

∆h = h′′ (r) + ( d − 1) cot r h ′(r). (587) Thus

5.4 Real-Space Adiabatic Algorithm on ℓp Balls 

Recently, Herman et al. [Her+25] introduced Real-Space Adiabatic Algorithm (RSAA), which is a dig-ital implementation of the quantum adiabatic algorithm to prepare the ground state of Schr ¨ odinger operators. Their algorithm uses the Dirichlet Laplacian as the kinetic operator and evolves the Hamiltonian in Euclidean space according to a sufficiently slow schedule. We extend this algo-rithm to a Riemannian manifolds by replacing the Euclidean Laplacian with the Laplace–Beltrami operator corresponding to the underlying metric g.While RsAA can be seen as more general ground state preparation algorithm, we will consider optimization problems. Let (M, g) be a compact connected Riemannian manifold, and let f :

M → R be a continuous potential function. Without loss of generality we can assume min M f = 0135 since shifting the potential by a constant only changes the evolution by a global phase. Let F be the operator 

(Fψ)( x) := f (x)ψ(x)

on L2(M).Fix an initial parameter λmin > 0 and a final parameter λmax > 0 and choose a twice con-tinuously differentiable, nondecreasing schedule α : [0, 1 ] → [λ2min , λ2max ] with α(0) = λ2min and 

α(1) = λ2max . Define the self-adjoint Hamiltonian 

Hg(s) := −∆g + α(s)F, 0 ≤ s ≤ 1. (649) The goal is to prepare the ground state of this Hamiltonian at s = 1 so that measurement yields a point ex ∈ M such that 

f (ex) − min  

> M

f ≤ ϵ,for some small ϵ > 0 with high probability. The algorithm then sets the parameters λ2min and λ2max so that preparation of the ground state at s = 0 is trivial and the ground state at s = 1 is sufficiently concentrated around the optimum. The evolution from s = 0 to s = 1 is then determined by the schedule α. The adiabatic theorem allows one to derive a simple schedule. Let E0(s) and E1(s) be its two lowest eigenvalues, counted with multiplicity, and define 

γ(s) := E1(s) − E0(s), γmin := min  

> s∈[0,1 ]

γ(s). (650) Assume that γmin > 0. Choose a normalized ground state ϕ0(s) satisfying 

Hg(s)ϕ0(s) = E0(s)ϕ0(s), ∥ϕ0(s)∥ = 1.

The other manifolds can be treated similarly by applying appropriate simulation results in previous sections with the appropriate boundary conditions and metrics. Since the Hamiltonian preserves the odd subspace, restricting this evolution to one odd sector recovers Dirichlet evolution. Let M = Ωball

= Hbox (s).Thus the two Hamiltonians have the same spectrum and the same spectral gap for every s ∈ [0, 1 ].Under the assumptions for rotated block-separable objectives in [Her+25, Theorem 3.6], Herman et al. show that this gap is inverse polynomial along the adiabatic path. The adiabatic runtime for Hbox (s) is therefore polynomial, and the same conclusion holds for its Duffy lift Hp,δ(s). For known black-box optimization algorithms, the runtime is exponential for optimizing the same class of functions. 

# AI Use Disclosure 

Large language model tools were used interactively at all stages of the preparation of this manuscript: from the earliest exploratory research stages to the final exposition and writing steps. The final manuscript was substantially revised by the authors, who remain solely responsible for all math-ematical claims, proofs, references, and conclusions. Most of the provable statements began as ideas from the author and GPT-5.6 Sol made substantial contributions to the analysis. GPT-5.6 Sol also generated some new ideas, notably, it suggested the use of the IMS Localization in the coherent-charts method.
